# The dry east: sagebrush steppe and ponderosa parkland, after Mark Ferrari — lessons

A student's log. Subject: big sagebrush, bitterbrush, bunchgrass (bluebunch wheatgrass), ponderosa
pine in open parkland, and the loess, soil crust and basalt they grow on.

**Code** (all in this folder; one index canvas, the palette is the light):

| file | what it holds |
|---|---|
| `sp.py` | the index canvas (material, step, depth band), clean runs for narrow forms, OKLab, sheet helpers |
| `palette.py` | ramps per material built from albedo x (sky ambient + sun), per season and hour; cool shadow / warm-lit hue shifts; depth-band haze |
| `sage.py` | big sagebrush (and bitterbrush): clumps, trunks, **grouped upright sprigs**, ragged foot, open crest |
| `grass.py` | Ferrari's paired blade (stage 0 copy) and the bunchgrass tussock at every size |
| `pine.py` | ponderosa: bark jigsaw (stretched Voronoi), limbs, tuft pads, starburst tufts, bristle rims |
| `world.py` | cameras (30° iso, standing perspective), world-space ground (loess, crust, pebbles, small grasses, snow) |
| `backdrop.py` | coulee rim (fluted basalt tiers over talus), far hills |
| `scene.py` | placement (clustered shrubs, grass in drifts, pines), LOD per plant by pixel size, cast shadows, contact, distance compression, far sage "sea", snow drifts, balsamroot |
| `sheets.py` | the parameter sheets (A–G) |
| `s0_tufts.py`, `s1_sage.py`, `s2_*.py`, `s3_pine.py`, `s4_*.py`, `s5_seasons.py`, `hero.py` | one script per stage |

Photos: `refs/` (fetched with the studio's imgfetch; each folder has a `_sheet.jpg`) plus the plant
desk's `~/work/naturalist/plants/photos/`. The ones I lean on: Hungate Canyon / Moses Coulee (sage in
low light before basalt), Seedskadee (sage flat with cured bunchgrass), Coulee City (a sage close up),
Sagebrush Hillside Morning Light (sage seen obliquely: the iso read), Turnbull NWR pine savanna
(eastern WA ponderosa), the 1939 Keen tree-class photos, and **`refs/sage_ds_all.png`**: photos
box-downsampled to 64 px, which is the scale we paint at. That last one was the most useful
instrument I built.

---

## Stage 0: copy Ferrari first

Ferrari never painted sage. The closest passages: **V11AM**'s dry grass tufts in front of the rock
(near-scale bunchgrass, backlit), V11AM's mesa (fluted cliff over a striated apron: the coulee rim),
**V16PM**'s golden bank (cured grass as a mass), and V16's conifers (dark mass, lit dabs on the tips).

![tufts idx](stage0/v11am_tuft_idx_8x.png)

**What his indices say** (`stage0/v11am_tuft_idx_8x.png`, false colour: black = his darkest, red =
mid, yellow = light): the tufts use **three indices only** (49/50/51 of V11AM). Every blade is a
**2-px stroke: a dark clean-run line with a 1-px light line on its sun side** (to the right of steep
runs, on top of shallow runs); the tip is the dark line alone; low on the tuft the light partner drops
to the mid index, and the base is a dense dark core. Blades arc: stiff at the base, the bend gathered
at the tip. Measured shares: grass covers 25% of the crop, of which 28% is the light index and 14% mid.

![copy](stage0/c1_tufts_v5b_4x.png)

The copy (`s0_tufts.py`, `grass.tuft_blades` + `draw_blade_pairs`) keeps his palette and a sketch
(the crop with grass removed, tuft centres from blurred density); every blade pixel is generated.
Light share 28% (his 28%), coverage 31% (his 25%).
**Not matched:** his blades are shorter and curl over sooner, so the tuft tops form a continuous
ragged fringe; a few of mine shoot as straight whips into the sky (the critic caught it, round 1).
His mid index is 14% of the grass, mine 3%: he uses the mid where blades cross and in the core, I
only use it low on each blade. I stopped here because the procedure was clear and the subject is
not his tufts; it is the thing I'd return to first.

## Stage 1: one sagebrush in greyscale

![grey](stage1/s1_sage_final_8x.png)

This is where the arc of the study happened. In order, what I built and why it failed:

1. **Spherical lobes on a trunk** (`stage1/s1_sage_a.png`): a little broccoli tree. Sage is not a
   tree: the foliage comes nearly to the ground, the trunk barely shows.
2. **Tall upright sprays from the crown down** (`s1_sage_c`): a weeping umbrella.
3. **Form field + scattered marks** (`s1_sage_i..k`): a legible mound, but the texture read as rain
   (even 1-px vertical ticks) and the base as scallops.
4. **Grouped sprigs** (now): the critic's round-3 note, and what `sage_ds_all.png` shows plainly:
   at 64 px a sage is **a heap of upright wedges 3–6 px wide, each lit on its rounded top and
   darkening downward, streaked inside, with dark gaps between where you see into the bush**.
   The sprig tops make the silhouette (the fringe); the gaps make it see-through; the interior
   (two dark values, darker low) is what shows in them.

Also learned here: **against the sky a sage is a tree on a stick; against ground it is a sage.**
From standing height the horizon is above a 1 m shrub, so its background is ground: the lit side
is a lost edge against the loess and the shadow side a found one. All my later plates put it on
ground with its cast shadow.

Two fixes that mattered: the foot is **ragged** (the interior's lower edge is cut per column and a
few stems run to the root crown, `sage._sprig_marks`), and **the crest is opened** (interior dark
left at the top of the silhouette reads as an outline; `sage._open_crest` removes it, so the edge
is sprig tops and gaps).

## Stage 2: colour, and the bush in its world

![side](stage2/s2_sage_side_final.png)
![iso](stage2/s2_sage_iso_final.png)

- **Palette** (`palette.py`): colour(step) = albedo × (occlusion(step) × sky + sun(step) × sun).
  Steps 0–2 are the shadow family (sky light only), 3–5 the lit family. The first version had a
  huge jump at the terminator and near-black cast shadows; steppe shadows are full of sky and
  bounce, so ambient went up.
- **Sage colour**: the first sage was mint (pale, cyan shadows). Photos at low sun: the shadow side
  goes **deep olive toward near-black**, the lit top pale grey-olive, warmed toward straw in warm
  light. Implemented as a shadow hue shift toward olive and a lit-end shift toward straw that grows
  with the sun's warmth (`SHADOW_SHIFT`, `LIGHT_SHIFT`).
- **Straw vs loess**: cured bunchgrass must be paler and yellower than the soil or the tussocks
  vanish into the ground; I had them within 5 levels of each other for a while.
- **The ground is calm** (critic rounds 2–3, and Ferrari's V16PM): flat lit loess, a slow
  undulation whose masses meet on a broken edge (not a dithered halo, not salt and pepper),
  sparse deliberate specks, crust patches that fade out through density, pebbles with a light top
  row, a dark foot and a one-pixel shadow.
- **Cast shadows** are the ground itself two steps down (the willow student's rule), projected from
  the sprite's silhouette along the sun, so their length follows the hour.

## The bunchgrass tussock

![grass](stage2/s2_grass_final.png)

V16PM's grass, read into a tussock: **a lit straw mass whose texture is carried by a few dark
strands** (22% of the blades, drawn over the mass in its lower 60%), the side away from the sun a
step down, a ragged dark foot along the ground line only. My earlier versions paired every lit
blade with a dark gap pixel (V11AM's stroke reversed for front light), which at 1:1 reads as a grey
comb; Ferrari's lit grass is mostly flat light with sparse darks. The same painter runs from 40 px
down to 6 px; under that a fountain dab; at 2–4 px a lit fleck over a darker foot; and in wide
views, small tussocks mostly merge into the ground's tone (a lit fleck with no foot and no
shadow, kept with a probability that ramps with size so the handoff doesn't pop: sheet F).
Placement: **grass grows in drifts** (a low-frequency field) and not against a shrub's foot
(critic round 3: "everything comes in pairs").

## Stage 3: ponderosa pine

![pine](stage3/s3_pine_final.png)

- **Bark**: orange plates split by black furrows, as a Voronoi stretched 3:1 vertically (furrow =
  near-equidistant pixels), lit across the cylinder. It is the ponderosa's signature and it reads.
- **Crown**: my first crowns were a coat rack (a few pads on bare limbs), then topiary (pads evenly
  stacked, critic round 3), then smooth blobs (too many big pads). What works: limb-end pads, plus
  fill pads clustered around the limbs with log-normal sizes, leaving sky holes; each pad a calm
  three-value base (shadow / reflected / lit halftone) with a dark underside, **starburst tufts** of
  the two lit steps whose density follows the light, and **bristles** (1–2 px needles) on the rims.
  The lit needles shift toward yellow-green (the photos' sun-caught tufts).
- **Small trees** (under ~90 px) get relatively bigger masses and fill the crown envelope directly,
  or they turn back into coat racks.
- Old trees are flat-topped (the upper limbs longest), young ones narrow with dark bark.

## Stage 4: in context — the coulee rim, the parkland

![rim](stage4/s4_rim_final.png)
![park](stage4/park_persp_final_3x.png)

- **The rim** (`backdrop.py`): big form first (the wall wanders in and out, so it is a sequence of
  lit and shadowed planes whose edges run slantwise), then V11AM-style fluting (irregular vertical
  light strokes paired with a dark one, strong in the light, faint in the shadow), benches with
  sage dots, a talus apron with fall-line streaks and cones under the gullies.
- **Distance** (critic round 3: "no distance"): sprites past the second depth band compress to two
  close values with no darkest index, the palette's haze lifts the black point and cools toward
  violet; past the distance where a sage is ~2 px wide the shrubs merge into the ground as
  horizontal flecks whose cover follows a big swale field, so the far flat has value masses; the
  horizon is broken by the rim or far hills.
- **Iso**: the sprite is painted in view space with the sun rotated by the camera's 30°; its
  height on screen is the plant's height × cos 30° plus half its depth × sin 30°. At 30° a
  mid-morning sun is mostly *front* light, so iso sage is lit over most of its face; the form then
  lives in the sprig tops and the gaps, not in a terminator.

## Stage 5: seasons, and the hero pictures

![seasons](stage5/seasons_final.png)

- **Spring**: green bunchgrass and sage a touch greener, **arrowleaf balsamroot** in clustered
  patches (a grey-green leaf mound with yellow heads).
- **Summer**: cured straw, seed stalks.
- **Autumn**: straw greyer; sage flower stalks.
- **Winter**: snow in lobed patches with a broken edge, **drifted against the lee side of each
  shrub and melted back on its sunny side** (critic round 5: ruled edges), blue shadows on snow, sage
  tops dusted.

![hero](hero/sage_flat_golden_3x.png)
![hero2](hero/parkland_morning_3x.png)
![hero3](hero/spring_balsamroot_3x.png)
![hero4](hero/winter_sage_3x.png)

## Sheets

`sheets.py` → `sheets/`:

- **A** season × hour (standing view, sage flat under a rim)
- **B** plant × size: sage, sage in autumn, bitterbrush, bunchgrass, pine young/mature/old, from 64 px
  down to 2 px (the level of detail; nothing vanishes)
- **C** sage density × seed (iso 30°)
- **D** landscape (sage flat, bunchgrass steppe, bitterbrush margin, pine parkland) × camera
  (standing, iso 16 px/m, iso 6 px/m)
- **E** sun azimuth × elevation (iso)
- **F** zoom ladder, iso, 48 → 3.3 px/m in 1.25× steps (for pops)
- **G** landscape × season (iso)

![A](sheets/A_season_x_hour.png)
![B](sheets/B_plant_x_size.png)
![C](sheets/C_density_x_seed.png)
![D](sheets/D_landscape_x_camera.png)
![E](sheets/E_sun_az_x_el.png)
![F](sheets/F_zoom_ladder.png)
![G](sheets/G_landscape_x_season.png)

Parameters (all in code): `sage.SageParams` (size, clumps, sprig width/fill, density, lean, stalks,
snow, sun), `grass.TussockParams` (size, spread, bend, stalks, dark-strand share), `pine.PineParams`
(height, crown fraction, width, age, fill, tufts, bristle), `world.GroundParams` (crust, stones,
litter, undulation, snow), `scene.DEFAULT_PLANTS` (densities per 100 m², sizes, clustering, grass
drift, backdrop), cameras `world.Iso(el, ppm)` and `world.Persp(eye, f, horizon)`, and
`palette.Palette(season, hour)`.

---

## Where it stands, honestly

**Ready, in my judgement** (and the critic's rounds 3–5):

- the standing-eye view of a sage flat at golden hour (`hero/sage_flat_golden`, `stage2/persp_*`):
  long shadows, warm loess, olive sage, straw tussocks, a far sea of sage under a hazy rim;
- the seasons (sheet A, `stage5/seasons_final`);
- the grouped-sprig sage from ~16 to ~110 px; the tussock from 6 to 40 px; ponderosa bark.

**Not there yet:**

- **The basalt rim.** Better than the fence it started as, but its tiers still read as horizontal
  bands and the columns are not yet prisms with a lit face and a dark face (critic round 4). The
  Moses Coulee photos show it is the whole read of that landscape; it deserves its own student
  (or its own master copy of V11AM's mesa, which I only looked at).
- **Pine crowns** read as ponderosa at 60–150 px, but mid-distance trees are a row of similar
  lollipops, and the crown's clumps are still more even than the Turnbull trees'.
- **The widest zoom** (3–5 px/m iso, sheet F bottom row) is busy: every sage is a dark dab with a
  shadow. The far-field rule from the standing view (merge into tone, value masses) should come to
  iso too.
- **Stage 0**: the tuft copy's blades are too long and too straight, and the mid index is
  underused.
- **Bitterbrush** is only a darker, greener, twiggier sage. It needs its own look: more open,
  irregular, with the stems showing and small leaf clusters.
- Ground specks in the single-plant plates are still louder than in the scenes.
- Speed: everything is Python loops; a 480×270 scene takes 10–40 s.

**What would help:** photos of sagebrush and ponderosa parkland taken from 20–40° above (a drone or
a hillside looking down), for the iso view; a set of the Moses Coulee colonnade in raking light, for
the rim; and bitterbrush close-ups (I found few).

Bash: Build photo | ours | Ferrari comparisons
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/steppe; python3 - <<'EOF'
from PIL import Image
import numpy as np, sp
P='~/work/naturalist/plants/photos/'
ph=Image.open(P+'Moses_Coulee_basalt/openverse_Hungate_Canyon_Moses_Coulee_Columbia_River_Plate_35216bab.jpg').convert('RGB')
ph=np.array(ph.crop((0,300,640,633)))
ours=np.array(Image.open('hero/sage_flat_golden_1x.png').convert('RGB'))[100:270,0:320]
fer=np.array(Image.open('~/work/pixelart-studies/pedagogy/master-copy/ref/lw/V16PM.png').convert('RGB'))[300:470,0:320]
H=510
row=sp.hstack([sp.fit(ph,H), sp.upscale(ours,3), sp.upscale(fer,3)],["photo: Hungate Canyon, sage in low sun","ours: hero/sage_flat_golden (3x)","Ferrari V16PM golden grass (3x)"])
sp.save(row,'stage5/compare_photo_ours_ferrari.png')
ph2=Image.open('refs/ponderosa_pine_savanna/commons_Turnbull_National_Wildlife_Refuge_pine_savanna_j_f9182dfb.jpg').convert('RGB')
ours2=np.array(Image.open('stage3/s3_pine_final.png').convert('RGB'))
fer2=np.array(Image.open('~/work/pixelart-studies/pedagogy/master-copy/ref/lw/V16.png').convert('RGB'))[0:250,0:160]
row2=sp.hstack([sp.fit(ph2,500), sp.fit(ours2[:, :1100],500), sp.upscale(fer2,2)],["photo: Turnbull NWR","ours: stage 3","Ferrari V16 conifers (2x)"])
sp.save(row2,'stage5/compare_pine.png')
EOF
echo ok

ok

Bash: Add comparison section to LESSONS
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/steppe; python3 - <<'EOF'
s=open('LESSONS.md').read()
s=s.replace('''## Sheets
''','''## Side by side: photo | ours | Ferrari

![cmp](stage5/compare_photo_ours_ferrari.png)
![cmp pine](stage5/compare_pine.png)

Read honestly: **ours is paler and more pastel than both.** Ferrari's V16PM grass is saturated
ochre with near-black olive strands, the whole bank a single strong light family; the Hungate
photo's sage in low sun is deep, saturated olive with warm tops. My golden hour lifts the darks
too much and keeps chroma low, so it reads as a hazy noon with long shadows. The next palette pass
should key the darks deeper and push chroma in the warm hours (the structure is right; the key is
timid). The pine comparison is closer in value; Ferrari's conifers are a dark mass with few, bright
lit dabs, mine spread the lit tufts more evenly.

## Sheets
''')
open('LESSONS.md','w').write(s)
EOF

SubagentHandback:

The steppe study is finished. Everything is in ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/steppe/, and LESSONS.md walks through each stage with photo | ours | Ferrari comparisons and an honest account of where it stands.

What works

  • Sagebrush (sage.py): four approaches failed before one held. What works is painting the bush as clumps of small upright sprigs, lit on top, with dark gaps where you see into the bush. The tool that settled it was shrinking real sage photos to the 64 px scale I paint at (refs/sage_ds_all.png). A sage also only reads as sage against ground; against sky it looks like a little tree on a stick.
  • Bunchgrass (grass.py): a lit straw fountain with a few dark strands, taken from Ferrari's golden grass bank (V16PM). It holds from 40 px down to a 2 px fleck that never disappears as the camera pulls back.
  • Ponderosa (pine.py): the orange plate-and-furrow bark reads well. Crowns are clumpy masses with lit needle tufts, in young, mature and old flat-topped forms.
  • The standing-eye view of a golden sage flat, and the four seasons. Spring has balsamroot; winter has snow drifted on the shady side of each shrub.

Not there yet

  • The basalt coulee rim still reads as horizontal bands, not columns.
  • Mid-distance pines repeat like lollipops.
  • The wide iso zoom (3–5 px/m) is busy with dark dots.
  • Bitterbrush is only a greener sage.
  • My warm-hour colours are too pastel next to both Ferrari and the photos; the darks need to go deeper.
  • The Ferrari copy I started from (the dry grass tufts in his V11AM scene) matches his share of light pixels but not his blade shape: mine are too long and straight.

Deliverables

  • Seven labelled parameter sheets in sheets/: season × hour, plant × size, density × seed, landscape × camera, sun angle, a zoom ladder, and landscape × season.
  • Four 480×270 scenes in hero/: golden sage flat, ponderosa parkland, spring balsamroot, winter.
  • The painters are Python modules with all parameters exposed; one script per stage regenerates everything.

A studio critic was already writing into my CRITIQUE.md, and I added one fresh-eyes critic of my own. Their notes drove most of the fixes.

Would help: sage and ponderosa photos taken from 20–40° above (for the iso view), the Moses Coulee basalt columns in raking light, and bitterbrush close-ups.
